From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is that I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m supposed to. It sounds silly saying it out loud, but it’s become really noticeable. Like, during client interviews for work, I’ll get up to “just grab something” or check something or pace a little, even when I really should be sitting there listening. And I do tutoring sometimes, and even there I’ll find myself popping up out of the chair when the other person is talking.\n\nIt’s not just work, either. I’m in a book club, and everyone else can sit with their tea and talk through the discussion, and I’m the one standing up, moving around, adjusting things, going to look at the shelf—anything except just staying put. People have started commenting on it, not always unkindly, but enough that I’m aware of it now. I just feel like I can’t sit still and listen the way other adults seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're sitting and that's why you get up, or is it more that you just can't seem to stay seated?\n\nPatient: It’s probably both, but the part that stands out is that I just don’t seem to stay seated when I’m expected to. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit and listen,” and then before I really register it, I’m up—getting a file, checking something across the room, moving my chair, refilling water, whatever excuse my brain comes up with in the moment.\n\nDuring client interviews it’s especially embarrassing because I know I’m supposed to look calm and attentive, and instead I’m sort of popping up and down like I can’t leave things alone. Same with tutoring—I should be sitting there while the other person works through something, but I keep finding reasons to stand up. And at book club, where there’s really no practical reason to be moving around, I’ll still end up wandering to the shelf or straightening things.\n\nSo, yes, there’s this uncomfortable “I need to move” feeling, but mostly what brings me in is that I keep leaving my seat when I’m supposed to stay put, and people are starting to notice that I can’t just sit and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're sitting and trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable when I’m trying to sit still, but it doesn’t feel like worry in the usual sense, like I’m sitting there thinking something terrible is going to happen. It’s more… physical, maybe? Like this pressure to move, or this feeling that staying still takes way more effort than it should.\n\nSometimes I probably look tense, and I might feel a little self-conscious if I notice people noticing me, but the actual reason I get up doesn’t feel like fear. It’s not like I’m panicking in the chair. It’s more that sitting there quietly feels almost impossible after a bit, and then I’m up before I’ve really decided to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what's being discussed when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve been thinking about the getting-up part so much that I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to whether I’m also missing what people are saying.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m forcing myself to stay still, I’m very aware of my body—like, “don’t move, don’t get up, just sit here”—and that can take up space in my head. But I don’t know that I can say clearly, yes, I can’t focus on the discussion. I’d have to think about it more. It’s more obvious to me that sitting still itself feels difficult, and then everything sort of revolves around that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really tracked it in that way. I know that when I’m trying to focus on something, I can get kind of caught up in the effort of staying with it, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m “easily distracted” in a clear, across-the-board way.\n\nLike, I’d have to think about what counts as distracted. If you mean, do I notice every little sound or suddenly lose the thread constantly, I’m not sure. I can’t honestly say I’ve paid enough attention to that to give you a clean yes. What I notice more is the struggle around sitting still or staying settled, and then sometimes it’s hard to tell whether my mind wandered because I was distracted, or because I was so aware of trying to keep myself in one place.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, maybe sometimes, but I don’t really know. It’s not the thing I’ve noticed most clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. Not always in some dramatic way, but I do jump in before I’ve really thought through whether it’s my place.\n\nAt work, for example, if I hear the junior detectives talking through a lead, I’ll suddenly be in the conversation with questions or suggestions before anyone asked me. It can feel like I’m just trying to help, and in the moment the thought is right there, like, “Wait, did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” and I’m saying it before I’ve paused to see if they actually wanted input.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. If I hear a neighbor talking in the hallway, I can kind of insert myself—ask a question, give advice, comment on something—and then a minute later I realize, oh, I just barged into someone else’s conversation. I don’t usually mean to be rude, but I can see how it comes across that way. It’s like the filter or the pause happens after I’ve already spoken, not before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsive behavior causing issues with your friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually the part that’s making me take it more seriously, because it’s not just, you know, “Oh, I talk too fast” or “I jump in sometimes.” It’s starting to cost me professionally.\n\nIn investigations, there have been times where I moved too quickly on what I thought was the important angle, and then later realized I’d missed a detail I should have caught. And that is… hard to admit, because my whole job depends on noticing details and listening carefully and not bulldozing past information just because I think I already see the shape of the thing.\n\nClients have gotten irritated with me too. Not always in a huge blowup way, but I can tell when I’ve interrupted too much or pushed them toward an answer before they were done explaining. Sometimes I’m trying to get to the relevant part, but from their side it probably feels like I’m rushing them or not really hearing them.\n\nAnd with junior detectives, that’s been uncomfortable. I’ve noticed some of them are less willing to debrief with me now. They’ll keep it shorter, or they’ll talk to someone else first, because I think they feel steamrolled by me. Like if they bring me a lead, I’m already questioning it, redirecting it, telling them what to do, before they’ve even laid out what they found. I don’t mean it that way, but I can see why they’d pull back.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing issues. Mostly at work, but work is a big part of my life, and it’s starting to feel like my “just trying to help” instinct is damaging trust.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I mean, I can sit with something for a while if I know exactly what I’m doing and why it matters, especially if there’s some urgency to it. But when you say “sustaining attention,” I’m not totally sure where the line is between normal fatigue or boredom and an actual problem.\n\nWith my work, tasks can be kind of uneven. Some parts pull you in because there’s a question to answer or a person to find or some inconsistency that keeps nagging at you. Other parts are just… sitting with records, reviewing notes, organizing information, going back over the same material again to make sure nothing was missed. And I know those parts can feel harder, but I don’t know if that means I have difficulty sustaining attention or if I just don’t like those parts as much.\n\nI haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, this happens often.” I notice more when I’m restless or when I’m pushing to get to the next piece of things. Whether that’s attention exactly, I don’t know. I guess I’d say I’m aware that some tasks feel harder to stay with than others, but I’m not confident enough to call it a major attention problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s one I notice a lot, especially when I’m forced to sit still at the computer for a long stretch.\n\nIf I’m combing through records or reading through pages of notes, my foot will be bouncing under the desk almost the whole time. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until the chair is kind of shaking or I notice my leg is tired. Or I’ll be clicking a pen, tapping it, shifting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs—just constantly adjusting myself.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is it can actually interfere with what I’m doing. I’ll be reading something important, and because I’m moving around or fidgeting with something in my hands, I lose my place on the page or on the screen. Then I have to go back and find where I was, and that makes the whole task take longer. It’s not like I’m trying to be restless; it just seems to happen when I’m stuck sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or acting on impulse when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, looking back, that was there pretty early. My grade-school report cards had that same kind of language over and over — “bright,” “capable,” “good ideas,” but also that I couldn’t stay in my seat, couldn’t wait my turn, was always up or blurting or moving before I was supposed to.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone framed it as anything serious. It was more like, “She’s energetic,” or “She needs to learn patience.” But my mother still jokes about it now. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, like I needed to get up and inspect everything instead of just sitting at my desk like the other kids.\n\nAnd I do remember that feeling, vaguely — not in a dramatic way, but that sitting still when I wanted to move or speak felt almost unbearable sometimes. Like if I had a thought, waiting for the right moment felt impossible. So yes, that part didn’t just appear recently. It was definitely there when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in severity over time?\n\nPatient: They’ve been there in some form for most of my life, but the severity definitely hasn’t been totally steady. Lately it’s been much more noticeable — I’d say for about the last eight months.\n\nThat lines up pretty closely with when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Since then, the sitting-still piece and the acting-before-I’ve-really-paused piece have felt harder to manage. It’s not that they appeared out of nowhere, because I can look back and see the same patterns earlier, but the volume got turned up after that move. Like I’m more aware of the restlessness now, and it feels more disruptive than it used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s like the filter or the pause happens after I’ve already spoken, not before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsive behavior causing issues with your friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it feels more like having too much energy in my body, honestly. Not necessarily good energy, just… constant motion, like my motor doesn’t really shut off.

On days when I don’t have field work, you’d think I’d be able to relax or at least sit at home and be off duty for a while, but I’ll still go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing case files to suddenly deciding I need to run three errands. It’s almost like I’m chasing a lead even when there isn’t one. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit down for a bit,” and then five minutes later I’m up doing something else because sitting there feels wrong somehow.

So yes, attention is probably part of it sometimes, but the staying-seated and impulse part feels more like this built-up drive to keep moving or act on whatever pops up next. I have a hard time just being still and off duty.

---------- turn 12 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is that I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m supposed to. It sounds silly saying it out loud, but it’s become really noticeable. Like, during client interviews for work, I’ll get up to “just grab something” or check something or pace a little, even when I really should be sitting there listening. And I do tutoring sometimes, and even there I’ll find myself popping up out of the chair when the other person is talking.\n\nIt’s not just work, either. I’m in a book club, and everyone else can sit with their tea and talk through the discussion, and I’m the one standing up, moving around, adjusting things, going to look at the shelf—anything except just staying put. People have started commenting on it, not always unkindly, but enough that I’m aware of it now. I just feel like I can’t sit still and listen the way other adults seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're sitting and that's why you get up, or is it more that you just can't seem to stay seated?\n\nPatient: It’s probably both, but the part that stands out is that I just don’t seem to stay seated when I’m expected to. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit and listen,” and then before I really register it, I’m up—getting a file, checking something across the room, moving my chair, refilling water, whatever excuse my brain comes up with in the moment.\n\nDuring client interviews it’s especially embarrassing because I know I’m supposed to look calm and attentive, and instead I’m sort of popping up and down like I can’t leave things alone. Same with tutoring—I should be sitting there while the other person works through something, but I keep finding reasons to stand up. And at book club, where there’s really no practical reason to be moving around, I’ll still end up wandering to the shelf or straightening things.\n\nSo, yes, there’s this uncomfortable “I need to move” feeling, but mostly what brings me in is that I keep leaving my seat when I’m supposed to stay put, and people are starting to notice that I can’t just sit and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're sitting and trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable when I’m trying to sit still, but it doesn’t feel like worry in the usual sense, like I’m sitting there thinking something terrible is going to happen. It’s more… physical, maybe? Like this pressure to move, or this feeling that staying still takes way more effort than it should.\n\nSometimes I probably look tense, and I might feel a little self-conscious if I notice people noticing me, but the actual reason I get up doesn’t feel like fear. It’s not like I’m panicking in the chair. It’s more that sitting there quietly feels almost impossible after a bit, and then I’m up before I’ve really decided to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what's being discussed when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve been thinking about the getting-up part so much that I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to whether I’m also missing what people are saying.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m forcing myself to stay still, I’m very aware of my body—like, “don’t move, don’t get up, just sit here”—and that can take up space in my head. But I don’t know that I can say clearly, yes, I can’t focus on the discussion. I’d have to think about it more. It’s more obvious to me that sitting still itself feels difficult, and then everything sort of revolves around that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really tracked it in that way. I know that when I’m trying to focus on something, I can get kind of caught up in the effort of staying with it, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m “easily distracted” in a clear, across-the-board way.\n\nLike, I’d have to think about what counts as distracted. If you mean, do I notice every little sound or suddenly lose the thread constantly, I’m not sure. I can’t honestly say I’ve paid enough attention to that to give you a clean yes. What I notice more is the struggle around sitting still or staying settled, and then sometimes it’s hard to tell whether my mind wandered because I was distracted, or because I was so aware of trying to keep myself in one place.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, maybe sometimes, but I don’t really know. It’s not the thing I’ve noticed most clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. Not always in some dramatic way, but I do jump in before I’ve really thought through whether it’s my place.\n\nAt work, for example, if I hear the junior detectives talking through a lead, I’ll suddenly be in the conversation with questions or suggestions before anyone asked me. It can feel like I’m just trying to help, and in the moment the thought is right there, like, “Wait, did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” and I’m saying it before I’ve paused to see if they actually wanted input.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. If I hear a neighbor talking in the hallway, I can kind of insert myself—ask a question, give advice, comment on something—and then a minute later I realize, oh, I just barged into someone else’s conversation. I don’t usually mean to be rude, but I can see how it comes across that way. It’s like the filter or the pause happens after I’ve already spoken, not before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsive behavior causing issues with your friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually the part that’s making me take it more seriously, because it’s not just, you know, “Oh, I talk too fast” or “I jump in sometimes.” It’s starting to cost me professionally.\n\nIn investigations, there have been times where I moved too quickly on what I thought was the important angle, and then later realized I’d missed a detail I should have caught. And that is… hard to admit, because my whole job depends on noticing details and listening carefully and not bulldozing past information just because I think I already see the shape of the thing.\n\nClients have gotten irritated with me too. Not always in a huge blowup way, but I can tell when I’ve interrupted too much or pushed them toward an answer before they were done explaining. Sometimes I’m trying to get to the relevant part, but from their side it probably feels like I’m rushing them or not really hearing them.\n\nAnd with junior detectives, that’s been uncomfortable. I’ve noticed some of them are less willing to debrief with me now. They’ll keep it shorter, or they’ll talk to someone else first, because I think they feel steamrolled by me. Like if they bring me a lead, I’m already questioning it, redirecting it, telling them what to do, before they’ve even laid out what they found. I don’t mean it that way, but I can see why they’d pull back.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing issues. Mostly at work, but work is a big part of my life, and it’s starting to feel like my “just trying to help” instinct is damaging trust.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I mean, I can sit with something for a while if I know exactly what I’m doing and why it matters, especially if there’s some urgency to it. But when you say “sustaining attention,” I’m not totally sure where the line is between normal fatigue or boredom and an actual problem.\n\nWith my work, tasks can be kind of uneven. Some parts pull you in because there’s a question to answer or a person to find or some inconsistency that keeps nagging at you. Other parts are just… sitting with records, reviewing notes, organizing information, going back over the same material again to make sure nothing was missed. And I know those parts can feel harder, but I don’t know if that means I have difficulty sustaining attention or if I just don’t like those parts as much.\n\nI haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, this happens often.” I notice more when I’m restless or when I’m pushing to get to the next piece of things. Whether that’s attention exactly, I don’t know. I guess I’d say I’m aware that some tasks feel harder to stay with than others, but I’m not confident enough to call it a major attention problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s one I notice a lot, especially when I’m forced to sit still at the computer for a long stretch.\n\nIf I’m combing through records or reading through pages of notes, my foot will be bouncing under the desk almost the whole time. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until the chair is kind of shaking or I notice my leg is tired. Or I’ll be clicking a pen, tapping it, shifting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs—just constantly adjusting myself.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is it can actually interfere with what I’m doing. I’ll be reading something important, and because I’m moving around or fidgeting with something in my hands, I lose my place on the page or on the screen. Then I have to go back and find where I was, and that makes the whole task take longer. It’s not like I’m trying to be restless; it just seems to happen when I’m stuck sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or acting on impulse when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, looking back, that was there pretty early. My grade-school report cards had that same kind of language over and over — “bright,” “capable,” “good ideas,” but also that I couldn’t stay in my seat, couldn’t wait my turn, was always up or blurting or moving before I was supposed to.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone framed it as anything serious. It was more like, “She’s energetic,” or “She needs to learn patience.” But my mother still jokes about it now. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, like I needed to get up and inspect everything instead of just sitting at my desk like the other kids.\n\nAnd I do remember that feeling, vaguely — not in a dramatic way, but that sitting still when I wanted to move or speak felt almost unbearable sometimes. Like if I had a thought, waiting for the right moment felt impossible. So yes, that part didn’t just appear recently. It was definitely there when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in severity over time?\n\nPatient: They’ve been there in some form for most of my life, but the severity definitely hasn’t been totally steady. Lately it’s been much more noticeable — I’d say for about the last eight months.\n\nThat lines up pretty closely with when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Since then, the sitting-still piece and the acting-before-I’ve-really-paused piece have felt harder to manage. It’s not that they appeared out of nowhere, because I can look back and see the same patterns earlier, but the volume got turned up after that move. Like I’m more aware of the restlessness now, and it feels more disruptive than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and acting on impulse are more related to having too much energy or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it feels more like having too much energy in my body, honestly. Not necessarily good energy, just… constant motion, like my motor doesn’t really shut off.\n\nOn days when I don’t have field work, you’d think I’d be able to relax or at least sit at home and be off duty for a while, but I’ll still go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing case files to suddenly deciding I need to run three errands. It’s almost like I’m chasing a lead even when there isn’t one. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit down for a bit,” and then five minutes later I’m up doing something else because sitting there feels wrong somehow.\n\nSo yes, attention is probably part of it sometimes, but the staying-seated and impulse part feels more like this built-up drive to keep moving or act on whatever pops up next. I have a hard time just being still and off duty.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more the too-much-energy, can’t-stay-still side of it. I mean, I can have focus when there’s something to pursue, but the bigger problem is that I don’t seem to know how to just stop.

Even on days when I don’t have field work, when there’s no real reason to be “on,” I’ll still bounce from one thing to another — unpacking boxes, then reorganizing case files, then suddenly deciding I need to run errands. It feels like I’m chasing a lead even when there isn’t one. Like my body is still in work mode and I can’t quite switch it off.

So I wouldn’t say it’s mainly that I can’t focus. It’s more that sitting still feels almost unnatural, and being off duty is weirdly hard for me.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is that I can’t seem to stay seated when I’m supposed to. It sounds silly saying it out loud, but it’s become really noticeable. Like, during client interviews for work, I’ll get up to “just grab something” or check something or pace a little, even when I really should be sitting there listening. And I do tutoring sometimes, and even there I’ll find myself popping up out of the chair when the other person is talking.\n\nIt’s not just work, either. I’m in a book club, and everyone else can sit with their tea and talk through the discussion, and I’m the one standing up, moving around, adjusting things, going to look at the shelf—anything except just staying put. People have started commenting on it, not always unkindly, but enough that I’m aware of it now. I just feel like I can’t sit still and listen the way other adults seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're sitting and that's why you get up, or is it more that you just can't seem to stay seated?\n\nPatient: It’s probably both, but the part that stands out is that I just don’t seem to stay seated when I’m expected to. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit and listen,” and then before I really register it, I’m up—getting a file, checking something across the room, moving my chair, refilling water, whatever excuse my brain comes up with in the moment.\n\nDuring client interviews it’s especially embarrassing because I know I’m supposed to look calm and attentive, and instead I’m sort of popping up and down like I can’t leave things alone. Same with tutoring—I should be sitting there while the other person works through something, but I keep finding reasons to stand up. And at book club, where there’s really no practical reason to be moving around, I’ll still end up wandering to the shelf or straightening things.\n\nSo, yes, there’s this uncomfortable “I need to move” feeling, but mostly what brings me in is that I keep leaving my seat when I’m supposed to stay put, and people are starting to notice that I can’t just sit and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're sitting and trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable when I’m trying to sit still, but it doesn’t feel like worry in the usual sense, like I’m sitting there thinking something terrible is going to happen. It’s more… physical, maybe? Like this pressure to move, or this feeling that staying still takes way more effort than it should.\n\nSometimes I probably look tense, and I might feel a little self-conscious if I notice people noticing me, but the actual reason I get up doesn’t feel like fear. It’s not like I’m panicking in the chair. It’s more that sitting there quietly feels almost impossible after a bit, and then I’m up before I’ve really decided to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what's being discussed when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve been thinking about the getting-up part so much that I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to whether I’m also missing what people are saying.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m forcing myself to stay still, I’m very aware of my body—like, “don’t move, don’t get up, just sit here”—and that can take up space in my head. But I don’t know that I can say clearly, yes, I can’t focus on the discussion. I’d have to think about it more. It’s more obvious to me that sitting still itself feels difficult, and then everything sort of revolves around that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really tracked it in that way. I know that when I’m trying to focus on something, I can get kind of caught up in the effort of staying with it, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m “easily distracted” in a clear, across-the-board way.\n\nLike, I’d have to think about what counts as distracted. If you mean, do I notice every little sound or suddenly lose the thread constantly, I’m not sure. I can’t honestly say I’ve paid enough attention to that to give you a clean yes. What I notice more is the struggle around sitting still or staying settled, and then sometimes it’s hard to tell whether my mind wandered because I was distracted, or because I was so aware of trying to keep myself in one place.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, maybe sometimes, but I don’t really know. It’s not the thing I’ve noticed most clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. Not always in some dramatic way, but I do jump in before I’ve really thought through whether it’s my place.\n\nAt work, for example, if I hear the junior detectives talking through a lead, I’ll suddenly be in the conversation with questions or suggestions before anyone asked me. It can feel like I’m just trying to help, and in the moment the thought is right there, like, “Wait, did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” and I’m saying it before I’ve paused to see if they actually wanted input.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. If I hear a neighbor talking in the hallway, I can kind of insert myself—ask a question, give advice, comment on something—and then a minute later I realize, oh, I just barged into someone else’s conversation. I don’t usually mean to be rude, but I can see how it comes across that way. It’s like the filter or the pause happens after I’ve already spoken, not before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsive behavior causing issues with your friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually the part that’s making me take it more seriously, because it’s not just, you know, “Oh, I talk too fast” or “I jump in sometimes.” It’s starting to cost me professionally.\n\nIn investigations, there have been times where I moved too quickly on what I thought was the important angle, and then later realized I’d missed a detail I should have caught. And that is… hard to admit, because my whole job depends on noticing details and listening carefully and not bulldozing past information just because I think I already see the shape of the thing.\n\nClients have gotten irritated with me too. Not always in a huge blowup way, but I can tell when I’ve interrupted too much or pushed them toward an answer before they were done explaining. Sometimes I’m trying to get to the relevant part, but from their side it probably feels like I’m rushing them or not really hearing them.\n\nAnd with junior detectives, that’s been uncomfortable. I’ve noticed some of them are less willing to debrief with me now. They’ll keep it shorter, or they’ll talk to someone else first, because I think they feel steamrolled by me. Like if they bring me a lead, I’m already questioning it, redirecting it, telling them what to do, before they’ve even laid out what they found. I don’t mean it that way, but I can see why they’d pull back.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing issues. Mostly at work, but work is a big part of my life, and it’s starting to feel like my “just trying to help” instinct is damaging trust.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I mean, I can sit with something for a while if I know exactly what I’m doing and why it matters, especially if there’s some urgency to it. But when you say “sustaining attention,” I’m not totally sure where the line is between normal fatigue or boredom and an actual problem.\n\nWith my work, tasks can be kind of uneven. Some parts pull you in because there’s a question to answer or a person to find or some inconsistency that keeps nagging at you. Other parts are just… sitting with records, reviewing notes, organizing information, going back over the same material again to make sure nothing was missed. And I know those parts can feel harder, but I don’t know if that means I have difficulty sustaining attention or if I just don’t like those parts as much.\n\nI haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, this happens often.” I notice more when I’m restless or when I’m pushing to get to the next piece of things. Whether that’s attention exactly, I don’t know. I guess I’d say I’m aware that some tasks feel harder to stay with than others, but I’m not confident enough to call it a major attention problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s one I notice a lot, especially when I’m forced to sit still at the computer for a long stretch.\n\nIf I’m combing through records or reading through pages of notes, my foot will be bouncing under the desk almost the whole time. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until the chair is kind of shaking or I notice my leg is tired. Or I’ll be clicking a pen, tapping it, shifting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs—just constantly adjusting myself.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is it can actually interfere with what I’m doing. I’ll be reading something important, and because I’m moving around or fidgeting with something in my hands, I lose my place on the page or on the screen. Then I have to go back and find where I was, and that makes the whole task take longer. It’s not like I’m trying to be restless; it just seems to happen when I’m stuck sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or acting on impulse when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, looking back, that was there pretty early. My grade-school report cards had that same kind of language over and over — “bright,” “capable,” “good ideas,” but also that I couldn’t stay in my seat, couldn’t wait my turn, was always up or blurting or moving before I was supposed to.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone framed it as anything serious. It was more like, “She’s energetic,” or “She needs to learn patience.” But my mother still jokes about it now. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, like I needed to get up and inspect everything instead of just sitting at my desk like the other kids.\n\nAnd I do remember that feeling, vaguely — not in a dramatic way, but that sitting still when I wanted to move or speak felt almost unbearable sometimes. Like if I had a thought, waiting for the right moment felt impossible. So yes, that part didn’t just appear recently. It was definitely there when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in severity over time?\n\nPatient: They’ve been there in some form for most of my life, but the severity definitely hasn’t been totally steady. Lately it’s been much more noticeable — I’d say for about the last eight months.\n\nThat lines up pretty closely with when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Since then, the sitting-still piece and the acting-before-I’ve-really-paused piece have felt harder to manage. It’s not that they appeared out of nowhere, because I can look back and see the same patterns earlier, but the volume got turned up after that move. Like I’m more aware of the restlessness now, and it feels more disruptive than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and acting on impulse are more related to having too much energy or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it feels more like having too much energy in my body, honestly. Not necessarily good energy, just… constant motion, like my motor doesn’t really shut off.\n\nOn days when I don’t have field work, you’d think I’d be able to relax or at least sit at home and be off duty for a while, but I’ll still go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing case files to suddenly deciding I need to run three errands. It’s almost like I’m chasing a lead even when there isn’t one. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit down for a bit,” and then five minutes later I’m up doing something else because sitting there feels wrong somehow.\n\nSo yes, attention is probably part of it sometimes, but the staying-seated and impulse part feels more like this built-up drive to keep moving or act on whatever pops up next. I’m in a book club, and everyone else can sit with their tea and talk through the discussion, and I’m the one standing up, moving around, adjusting things, going to look at the shelf—anything except just staying put. People have started commenting on it, not always unkindly, but enough that I’m aware of it now. I just feel like I can’t sit still and listen the way other adults seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you're sitting and that's why you get up, or is it more that you just can't seem to stay seated?\n\nPatient: It’s probably both, but the part that stands out is that I just don’t seem to stay seated when I’m expected to. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit and listen,” and then before I really register it, I’m up—getting a file, checking something across the room, moving my chair, refilling water, whatever excuse my brain comes up with in the moment.\n\nDuring client interviews it’s especially embarrassing because I know I’m supposed to look calm and attentive, and instead I’m sort of popping up and down like I can’t leave things alone. Same with tutoring—I should be sitting there while the other person works through something, but I keep finding reasons to stand up. And at book club, where there’s really no practical reason to be moving around, I’ll still end up wandering to the shelf or straightening things.\n\nSo, yes, there’s this uncomfortable “I need to move” feeling, but mostly what brings me in is that I keep leaving my seat when I’m supposed to stay put, and people are starting to notice that I can’t just sit and listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're sitting and trying to stay still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxiety, exactly. I mean, I do get uncomfortable when I’m trying to sit still, but it doesn’t feel like worry in the usual sense, like I’m sitting there thinking something terrible is going to happen. It’s more… physical, maybe? Like this pressure to move, or this feeling that staying still takes way more effort than it should.\n\nSometimes I probably look tense, and I might feel a little self-conscious if I notice people noticing me, but the actual reason I get up doesn’t feel like fear. It’s not like I’m panicking in the chair. It’s more that sitting there quietly feels almost impossible after a bit, and then I’m up before I’ve really decided to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what's being discussed when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve been thinking about the getting-up part so much that I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to whether I’m also missing what people are saying.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m forcing myself to stay still, I’m very aware of my body—like, “don’t move, don’t get up, just sit here”—and that can take up space in my head. But I don’t know that I can say clearly, yes, I can’t focus on the discussion. I’d have to think about it more. It’s more obvious to me that sitting still itself feels difficult, and then everything sort of revolves around that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really tracked it in that way. I know that when I’m trying to focus on something, I can get kind of caught up in the effort of staying with it, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m “easily distracted” in a clear, across-the-board way.\n\nLike, I’d have to think about what counts as distracted. If you mean, do I notice every little sound or suddenly lose the thread constantly, I’m not sure. I can’t honestly say I’ve paid enough attention to that to give you a clean yes. What I notice more is the struggle around sitting still or staying settled, and then sometimes it’s hard to tell whether my mind wandered because I was distracted, or because I was so aware of trying to keep myself in one place.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, maybe sometimes, but I don’t really know. It’s not the thing I’ve noticed most clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. Not always in some dramatic way, but I do jump in before I’ve really thought through whether it’s my place.\n\nAt work, for example, if I hear the junior detectives talking through a lead, I’ll suddenly be in the conversation with questions or suggestions before anyone asked me. It can feel like I’m just trying to help, and in the moment the thought is right there, like, “Wait, did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” and I’m saying it before I’ve paused to see if they actually wanted input.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. If I hear a neighbor talking in the hallway, I can kind of insert myself—ask a question, give advice, comment on something—and then a minute later I realize, oh, I just barged into someone else’s conversation. I don’t usually mean to be rude, but I can see how it comes across that way. It’s like the filter or the pause happens after I’ve already spoken, not before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsive behavior causing issues with your friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually the part that’s making me take it more seriously, because it’s not just, you know, “Oh, I talk too fast” or “I jump in sometimes.” It’s starting to cost me professionally.\n\nIn investigations, there have been times where I moved too quickly on what I thought was the important angle, and then later realized I’d missed a detail I should have caught. And that is… hard to admit, because my whole job depends on noticing details and listening carefully and not bulldozing past information just because I think I already see the shape of the thing.\n\nClients have gotten irritated with me too. Not always in a huge blowup way, but I can tell when I’ve interrupted too much or pushed them toward an answer before they were done explaining. Sometimes I’m trying to get to the relevant part, but from their side it probably feels like I’m rushing them or not really hearing them.\n\nAnd with junior detectives, that’s been uncomfortable. I’ve noticed some of them are less willing to debrief with me now. They’ll keep it shorter, or they’ll talk to someone else first, because I think they feel steamrolled by me. Like if they bring me a lead, I’m already questioning it, redirecting it, telling them what to do, before they’ve even laid out what they found. I don’t mean it that way, but I can see why they’d pull back.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing issues. Mostly at work, but work is a big part of my life, and it’s starting to feel like my “just trying to help” instinct is damaging trust.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I mean, I can sit with something for a while if I know exactly what I’m doing and why it matters, especially if there’s some urgency to it. But when you say “sustaining attention,” I’m not totally sure where the line is between normal fatigue or boredom and an actual problem.\n\nWith my work, tasks can be kind of uneven. Some parts pull you in because there’s a question to answer or a person to find or some inconsistency that keeps nagging at you. Other parts are just… sitting with records, reviewing notes, organizing information, going back over the same material again to make sure nothing was missed. And I know those parts can feel harder, but I don’t know if that means I have difficulty sustaining attention or if I just don’t like those parts as much.\n\nI haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, this happens often.” I notice more when I’m restless or when I’m pushing to get to the next piece of things. Whether that’s attention exactly, I don’t know. I guess I’d say I’m aware that some tasks feel harder to stay with than others, but I’m not confident enough to call it a major attention problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s one I notice a lot, especially when I’m forced to sit still at the computer for a long stretch.\n\nIf I’m combing through records or reading through pages of notes, my foot will be bouncing under the desk almost the whole time. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until the chair is kind of shaking or I notice my leg is tired. Or I’ll be clicking a pen, tapping it, shifting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs—just constantly adjusting myself.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is it can actually interfere with what I’m doing. I’ll be reading something important, and because I’m moving around or fidgeting with something in my hands, I lose my place on the page or on the screen. Then I have to go back and find where I was, and that makes the whole task take longer. It’s not like I’m trying to be restless; it just seems to happen when I’m stuck sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or acting on impulse when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, looking back, that was there pretty early. My grade-school report cards had that same kind of language over and over — “bright,” “capable,” “good ideas,” but also that I couldn’t stay in my seat, couldn’t wait my turn, was always up or blurting or moving before I was supposed to.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone framed it as anything serious. It was more like, “She’s energetic,” or “She needs to learn patience.” But my mother still jokes about it now. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, like I needed to get up and inspect everything instead of just sitting at my desk like the other kids.\n\nAnd I do remember that feeling, vaguely — not in a dramatic way, but that sitting still when I wanted to move or speak felt almost unbearable sometimes. Like if I had a thought, waiting for the right moment felt impossible. So yes, that part didn’t just appear recently. It was definitely there when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in severity over time?\n\nPatient: They’ve been there in some form for most of my life, but the severity definitely hasn’t been totally steady. Lately it’s been much more noticeable — I’d say for about the last eight months.\n\nThat lines up pretty closely with when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Since then, the sitting-still piece and the acting-before-I’ve-really-paused piece have felt harder to manage. It’s not that they appeared out of nowhere, because I can look back and see the same patterns earlier, but the volume got turned up after that move. Like I’m more aware of the restlessness now, and it feels more disruptive than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and acting on impulse are more related to having too much energy or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it feels more like having too much energy in my body, honestly. Not necessarily good energy, just… constant motion, like my motor doesn’t really shut off.\n\nOn days when I don’t have field work, you’d think I’d be able to relax or at least sit at home and be off duty for a while, but I’ll still go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing case files to suddenly deciding I need to run three errands. It’s almost like I’m chasing a lead even when there isn’t one. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit down for a bit,” and then five minutes later I’m up doing something else because sitting there feels wrong somehow.\n\nSo yes, attention is probably part of it sometimes, but the staying-seated and impulse part feels more like this built-up drive to keep moving or act on whatever pops up next. I have a hard time just being still and off duty.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus or that you have too much energy and can't stay still?\n\nPatient: It’s more the too-much-energy, can’t-stay-still side of it. I mean, I can have focus when there’s something to pursue, but the bigger problem is that I don’t seem to know how to just stop.\n\nEven on days when I don’t have field work, when there’s no real reason to be “on,” I’ll still bounce from one thing to another — unpacking boxes, then reorganizing case files, then suddenly deciding I need to run errands. It feels like I’m chasing a lead even when there isn’t one. Like my body is still in work mode and I can’t quite switch it off.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s mainly that I can’t focus. It’s more that sitting still feels almost unnatural, and being off duty is weirdly hard for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s definitely more the overly active and restless part. I can pay attention when there’s something concrete in front of me, especially if it feels urgent or interesting, but the thing that really wears me down is that I don’t seem to know how to be still.

Like, even on days when I don’t have field work — when technically I should be able to take the day slower — I’ll still keep moving from one thing to the next. I’ll start unpacking boxes, then suddenly I’m reorganizing case files, then I’m out the door running errands, and the whole time it feels like I’m chasing a lead even though there isn’t actually a lead to chase.

It’s like my body stays in work mode. I have a really hard time just being off duty. Sitting down and doing nothing feels almost uncomfortable, like I’m supposed to be getting up or handling something. So I’d say the main issue is the constant activity and restlessness, more than just not being able to pay attention.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I’m in a book club, and everyone else can sit with their tea and talk through the discussion, and I’m the one standing up, moving around, adjusting things, going to look at the shelf—anything except just staying put. I mean, I do get uncomfortable when I’m trying to sit still, but it doesn’t feel like worry in the usual sense, like I’m sitting there thinking something terrible is going to happen. It’s more… physical, maybe? Like this pressure to move, or this feeling that staying still takes way more effort than it should.\n\nSometimes I probably look tense, and I might feel a little self-conscious if I notice people noticing me, but the actual reason I get up doesn’t feel like fear. It’s not like I’m panicking in the chair. It’s more that sitting there quietly feels almost impossible after a bit, and then I’m up before I’ve really decided to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on what's being discussed when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I’ve been thinking about the getting-up part so much that I don’t know if I’ve paid enough attention to whether I’m also missing what people are saying.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I’m forcing myself to stay still, I’m very aware of my body—like, “don’t move, don’t get up, just sit here”—and that can take up space in my head. But I don’t know that I can say clearly, yes, I can’t focus on the discussion. I’d have to think about it more. It’s more obvious to me that sitting still itself feels difficult, and then everything sort of revolves around that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted when you're trying to focus on something?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I haven’t really tracked it in that way. I know that when I’m trying to focus on something, I can get kind of caught up in the effort of staying with it, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m “easily distracted” in a clear, across-the-board way.\n\nLike, I’d have to think about what counts as distracted. If you mean, do I notice every little sound or suddenly lose the thread constantly, I’m not sure. I can’t honestly say I’ve paid enough attention to that to give you a clean yes. What I notice more is the struggle around sitting still or staying settled, and then sometimes it’s hard to tell whether my mind wandered because I was distracted, or because I was so aware of trying to keep myself in one place.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is, maybe sometimes, but I don’t really know. It’s not the thing I’ve noticed most clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably fair to say. Not always in some dramatic way, but I do jump in before I’ve really thought through whether it’s my place.\n\nAt work, for example, if I hear the junior detectives talking through a lead, I’ll suddenly be in the conversation with questions or suggestions before anyone asked me. It can feel like I’m just trying to help, and in the moment the thought is right there, like, “Wait, did you check this?” or “No, ask it this way,” and I’m saying it before I’ve paused to see if they actually wanted input.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. If I hear a neighbor talking in the hallway, I can kind of insert myself—ask a question, give advice, comment on something—and then a minute later I realize, oh, I just barged into someone else’s conversation. I don’t usually mean to be rude, but I can see how it comes across that way. It’s like the filter or the pause happens after I’ve already spoken, not before.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your impulsive behavior causing issues with your friends, family, or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually the part that’s making me take it more seriously, because it’s not just, you know, “Oh, I talk too fast” or “I jump in sometimes.” It’s starting to cost me professionally.\n\nIn investigations, there have been times where I moved too quickly on what I thought was the important angle, and then later realized I’d missed a detail I should have caught. And that is… hard to admit, because my whole job depends on noticing details and listening carefully and not bulldozing past information just because I think I already see the shape of the thing.\n\nClients have gotten irritated with me too. Not always in a huge blowup way, but I can tell when I’ve interrupted too much or pushed them toward an answer before they were done explaining. Sometimes I’m trying to get to the relevant part, but from their side it probably feels like I’m rushing them or not really hearing them.\n\nAnd with junior detectives, that’s been uncomfortable. I’ve noticed some of them are less willing to debrief with me now. They’ll keep it shorter, or they’ll talk to someone else first, because I think they feel steamrolled by me. Like if they bring me a lead, I’m already questioning it, redirecting it, telling them what to do, before they’ve even laid out what they found. I don’t mean it that way, but I can see why they’d pull back.\n\nSo yes, it’s causing issues. Mostly at work, but work is a big part of my life, and it’s starting to feel like my “just trying to help” instinct is damaging trust.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining attention on tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I mean, I can sit with something for a while if I know exactly what I’m doing and why it matters, especially if there’s some urgency to it. But when you say “sustaining attention,” I’m not totally sure where the line is between normal fatigue or boredom and an actual problem.\n\nWith my work, tasks can be kind of uneven. Some parts pull you in because there’s a question to answer or a person to find or some inconsistency that keeps nagging at you. Other parts are just… sitting with records, reviewing notes, organizing information, going back over the same material again to make sure nothing was missed. And I know those parts can feel harder, but I don’t know if that means I have difficulty sustaining attention or if I just don’t like those parts as much.\n\nI haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, this happens often.” I notice more when I’m restless or when I’m pushing to get to the next piece of things. Whether that’s attention exactly, I don’t know. I guess I’d say I’m aware that some tasks feel harder to stay with than others, but I’m not confident enough to call it a major attention problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s one I notice a lot, especially when I’m forced to sit still at the computer for a long stretch.\n\nIf I’m combing through records or reading through pages of notes, my foot will be bouncing under the desk almost the whole time. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until the chair is kind of shaking or I notice my leg is tired. Or I’ll be clicking a pen, tapping it, shifting in the chair, crossing and uncrossing my legs—just constantly adjusting myself.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is it can actually interfere with what I’m doing. I’ll be reading something important, and because I’m moving around or fidgeting with something in my hands, I lose my place on the page or on the screen. Then I have to go back and find where I was, and that makes the whole task take longer. It’s not like I’m trying to be restless; it just seems to happen when I’m stuck sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble sitting still or acting on impulse when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, looking back, that was there pretty early. My grade-school report cards had that same kind of language over and over — “bright,” “capable,” “good ideas,” but also that I couldn’t stay in my seat, couldn’t wait my turn, was always up or blurting or moving before I was supposed to.\n\nAt the time I don’t think anyone framed it as anything serious. It was more like, “She’s energetic,” or “She needs to learn patience.” But my mother still jokes about it now. She says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, like I needed to get up and inspect everything instead of just sitting at my desk like the other kids.\n\nAnd I do remember that feeling, vaguely — not in a dramatic way, but that sitting still when I wanted to move or speak felt almost unbearable sometimes. Like if I had a thought, waiting for the right moment felt impossible. So yes, that part didn’t just appear recently. It was definitely there when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and impulsivity consistently throughout your life, or have they varied in severity over time?\n\nPatient: They’ve been there in some form for most of my life, but the severity definitely hasn’t been totally steady. Lately it’s been much more noticeable — I’d say for about the last eight months.\n\nThat lines up pretty closely with when I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. Since then, the sitting-still piece and the acting-before-I’ve-really-paused piece have felt harder to manage. It’s not that they appeared out of nowhere, because I can look back and see the same patterns earlier, but the volume got turned up after that move. Like I’m more aware of the restlessness now, and it feels more disruptive than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty staying seated and acting on impulse are more related to having too much energy or having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think it feels more like having too much energy in my body, honestly. Not necessarily good energy, just… constant motion, like my motor doesn’t really shut off.\n\nOn days when I don’t have field work, you’d think I’d be able to relax or at least sit at home and be off duty for a while, but I’ll still go from unpacking boxes to reorganizing case files to suddenly deciding I need to run three errands. It’s almost like I’m chasing a lead even when there isn’t one. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just sit down for a bit,” and then five minutes later I’m up doing something else because sitting there feels wrong somehow.\n\nSo yes, attention is probably part of it sometimes, but the staying-seated and impulse part feels more like this built-up drive to keep moving or act on whatever pops up next. I have a hard time just being still and off duty.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is that you can't focus or that you have too much energy and can't stay still?\n\nPatient: It’s more the too-much-energy, can’t-stay-still side of it. I mean, I can have focus when there’s something to pursue, but the bigger problem is that I don’t seem to know how to just stop.\n\nEven on days when I don’t have field work, when there’s no real reason to be “on,” I’ll still bounce from one thing to another — unpacking boxes, then reorganizing case files, then suddenly deciding I need to run errands. It feels like I’m chasing a lead even when there isn’t one. Like my body is still in work mode and I can’t quite switch it off.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s mainly that I can’t focus. It’s more that sitting still feels almost unnatural, and being off duty is weirdly hard for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main issue is more with not being able to pay attention or with being overly active and restless?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more the overly active and restless part. I can pay attention when there’s something concrete in front of me, especially if it feels urgent or interesting, but the thing that really wears me down is that I don’t seem to know how to be still.\n\nLike, even on days when I don’t have field work — when technically I should be able to take the day slower — I’ll still keep moving from one thing to the next. I’ll start unpacking boxes, then suddenly I’m reorganizing case files, then I’m out the door running errands, and the whole time it feels like I’m chasing a lead even though there isn’t actually a lead to chase.\n\nIt’s like my body stays in work mode. I have a really hard time just being off duty. Sitting down and doing nothing feels almost uncomfortable, like I’m supposed to be getting up or handling something. So I’d say the main issue is the constant activity and restlessness, more than just not being able to pay attention.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F90.0", "F90.8", "F90.9"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I have a really hard time just being off duty. Sitting down and doing nothing feels almost uncomfortable, like I’m supposed to be getting up or handling something. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, I don’t like admitting that, because in my line of work you’re supposed to be sharp, measured, good at listening and putting pieces together. But it has started to cost me professionally.

There have been investigations where I’ve moved too fast — jumped in, acted on the next thing that occurred to me, pushed ahead before I’d really sat with all the details — and later I realized I missed something I should have caught. Not huge dramatic TV-type mistakes, but enough that it mattered. A detail in a statement, a timing issue, something in a file that I skimmed because I was already halfway out the door mentally and physically.

Clients notice too. I can get impatient in meetings, or I’ll interrupt because I think I know where they’re going, and sometimes they get irritated. I can tell when someone feels like I’m not giving them enough room, even if I’m trying to be helpful or efficient.

And with junior detectives, that’s been harder than I expected. Some of them don’t seem as willing to debrief with me now, because I think they feel steamrolled. I ask questions fast, I cut in, I’m already moving to the next angle before they’ve finished laying out what they saw. I don’t mean to shut them down, but I can see how it lands.

So yes, it’s not just annoying or embarrassing. It’s affecting the way I work with people and the quality of the work itself.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic.
